From 2012 to 2018 in the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), the eastern Golan Heights became a scene of repeated battles between the [Syrian Army](3423927.md), rebel factions of the [Syrian opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_opposition_\(2011%E2%80%932024\)) including the moderate [Southern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Front_\(Syrian_rebel_group\)), the jihadist [al-Nusra Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nusra_Front), and [factions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_ibn_al-Walid_Army) affiliated with the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) (ISIL) terrorist group.[^cite_note-265]

The atrocities of the Syrian civil war and the rise of ISIL, which from 2016 to 2018 controlled parts of the Syrian-administered Golan, added a new twist to the issue. In 2015, it was reported that Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) asked US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) to recognize Israeli claims to the territory because of these recent ISIL actions and because he said that modern Syria had likely "disintegrated" beyond the point of reunification.[^cite_note-266] The [White House](33057.md) dismissed Netanyahu's suggestion, stating that President Obama continued to support UN resolutions 242 and 497, and any alterations of this policy could strain American alliances with Western-backed Syrian rebel groups.[^cite_note-Haaretz-267]

In 2016, the [Islamic State](9087364.md) apologized to Israel after a firefight with Israeli soldiers in the area.[^cite_note-268]

After the [April 2018 missile strikes against Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2018_missile_strikes_against_Syria) by the United States, France, and the United Kingdom, a group of about 500 [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) in the Golan Heights town of [Ein Qiniyye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_Qiniyye) marched in support of Syrian president [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) on Syria's [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Syria\)) and in condemnation of the American-led strikes.[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270]

In May 2018, after 20 Iranian rockets were launched at Israeli army positions in the western Golan Heights, the first direct attack by Iran against Israeli forces, the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md) (IDF) launched [Operation House of Cards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_House_of_Cards), a series of "extensive" air strikes against [Iranian](1285472.md) [military installations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_installations) in Syria.[^cite_note-271]

After waging a month-long [2018 Southern Syria offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Southern_Syria_offensive) against the rebels and ISIL, the Syrian government regained control of the eastern Golan Heights on 31 July 2018.[^cite_note-boot-38]

### Gaza war spillover

Further information: [2024 Israeli invasion of Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Israeli_invasion_of_Lebanon)

The day after the [October 7 attacks](74998317.md), [Hezbollah](13919.md) fired mortars at Northern Israel from Lebanese territory, and Israel responded with artillery fire.[^cite_note-272] [Hostilities are still ongoing](75005425.md). In the first year following the attacks, Hezbollah fired 8,000 rockets into Israel, resulting in the displacement of 70,000 Israelis.[^cite_note-273] During the same period, Israeli reprisals resulted in the displacement of 1.4 million Lebanese people.[^cite_note-274]

In June 2024, after a wave of Israeli strikes on Hezbollah targets in Lebanon, Hezbollah launched a series of rocket and drone attacks in the Golan Heights, causing the destruction of 10,000 dunams (10 km<sup>2</sup>; 3.9 sq mi) of open areas by fire and damaging parts of the [Yehudiya Forest Nature Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yehudiya_Forest_Nature_Reserve), including hiking trails and the reserve's Black Canyon. An official from the [Israel Nature and Parks Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Nature_and_Parks_Authority) said it would take years for the local flora to recover.[^cite_note-275]

A rocket fired from Lebanese territory [struck a soccer field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams_attack) in the Druze town of [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams) in the Golan Heights on July 27, 2024.[^cite_note-276] The strike resulted in the deaths of 12 Druze children. The IDF stated the rocket was fired by Hezbollah, a claim which Hezbollah denied.[^cite_note-277] US intelligence could not determine if the attack was intentional or a misfire, but they maintained that Hezbollah was responsible.[^cite_note-APNews20240728-278] Meanwhile, Hezbollah, along with Iranian and Qatari state media, posited that an Israeli [Iron Dome](18342427.md) interceptor was to blame.[^cite_note-TheGuardian20240728-279][^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282]

### 2024 Israeli invasion of Syria

Further information: [Israeli invasion of Syria (2024–present)](78549398.md)

Following the [2024 Syrian opposition offensives](78460726.md) and the [fall of the Assad regime](78548066.md), Prime Minister Netanyahu ordered Israeli forces to seize the buffer zone on 8 December 2024, citing the abandonment of Syrian positions and the collapse of the 1974 ceasefire agreement.[^cite_note-283] Israeli forces also launched strikes on Syrian military assets, including air strikes destroying the [Syrian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Navy) and, it was claimed, 90% of Syria's known surface-to-air missiles.[^cite_note-Krever-284]

Israel started violating the 1974 Disengagement Agreement before Assad's fall in November with engineering work and battle tanks inside the demilitarized zone.[^cite_note-e751-285] UNDOF had: "repeatedly engaged with the IDF to protest the construction"[^cite_note-e751-285] In December, Israeli forces occupied [Mount Hermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon) advancing as far as the town of [Beqaasem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beqaasem), situated about 25 kilometres (16 mi) from Damascus. Holding Mount Hermon - at 2,800 metres (9,200 ft) the highest point in Syria - would facilitate Israeli electronic surveillance deep in Syrian territory and provide additional warning with respect to military developments in the region.[^cite_note-Krever-284]

A report from June 2025, showed that since the Israeli invasion of Syria in 2024, the Israelis have deforested large portions of the Quneitra governorate.[^cite_note-Khalaf-286] Hundreds of acres of trees have been cut down by Israel including the Jabaatha Nature Reserve and Kudna forest have been destroyed.[^cite_note-Khalaf-286] The deforestation threatens wildlife and biodiversity in the region.[^cite_note-Khalaf-286] Syrian farmers have also been prohibited from entering their farmlands.[^cite_note-Khalaf-286]

In January 2026, the Israeli military destroyed two historic buildings in the Golan, the al-Golan hospital and al-Andalus Cinema, both in [Quneitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra).[^cite_note-p814-287][^cite_note-288]

In January 2026, several reports documented Israeli planes spraying chemicals on Syrian farmland and forests in the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-t957-289][^cite_note-i867-290][^cite_note-i439-291][^cite_note-d389-292] Israel spraying chemicals were also reported in southern Lebanon.[^cite_note-u485-293] A sample from Lebanon showed that the chemical contained a [carcinogenic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carcinogen) classified herbicide, but in dozens of times higher concentration than normal usage.[^cite_note-u485-293][^cite_note-t957-289]

In July 2026, the United Nations Secretary-General [António Guterres](471806.md) visited Syria and stated: "Violations of Syria's sovereignty and territorial integrity are unacceptable and must cease. What is happening in the region around the Golan Heights is totally unacceptable," and: "I'd like to remind the international community that the Golan Heights are Syrian territory."[^cite_note-n842-294]

## Geography



[Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee) and southern Golan Heights, viewed from [Umm Qais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Qais) and the ruins of [Gadara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadara) in [Jordan](7515964.md)

### Geology



1994 [CIA](5183633.md) map of Golan Heights and vicinity

The plateau that Israel controls is part of a larger geological unit of volcanic [basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) fields stretching north and east into north Jordan and southern Syria, past the Syrian desert,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbbasiRaphael2004297-295] that were formed by a series of volcanic eruptions that began about 4 million years ago.[^cite_note-296] The rock forming the mountainous area in the northern Golan Heights, descending from Mount Hermon, differs geologically from the volcanic rocks of the plateau and has a different [physiography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiography). The mountains are characterised by lighter-colored, [Jurassic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic)-age [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone) of [sedimentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary) origin. Locally, the limestone is broken by [faults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fault_\(geology\)) and solution channels to form a [karst-like topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karst_topography) in which springs are common. , [Geologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology), the Golan plateau and the [Hauran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauran) [plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain) to the east constitute a [Holocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene) [volcanic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_field) that also extends northeast almost to [Damascus](8914.md). Much of the area is scattered with [dormant volcanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormant_volcano), as well as [cinder cones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_cones#Cinder_cone), such as [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams). The plateau also contains a [crater lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_crater_lake), called [Birkat Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkat_Ram) ("Ram Pool"), which is fed by both [surface runoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_runoff) and underground springs. These volcanic areas are characterised by [basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) bedrock and dark soils derived from its [weathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weathering). The basalt flows overlie older, distinctly lighter-colored [limestones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestones) and [marls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marl), exposed along the Yarmouk River in the south.

### Boundaries

The geographic definition of the Golan varies but is generally defined as the area bound by the [Jordan Valley](12105764.md) to the west, which separates it from the [Galilee](12639.md) in Israel, the [Yarmouk River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarmouk_River) to the south, which separates it from the [Jabal Ajlun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_Ajlun) region in Jordan, and the Sa'ar stream (a tributary of Nahal Hermon/Nahr Baniyas) to the north which separates it from [Mount Hermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon) and the [Hula Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hula_Valley) close to the border with Lebanon. The natural eastern boundary of the region is alternatively placed at the [Ruqqad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruqqad) river or the [Allan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahr_al-Allan) river further east, which separates the Golan from the [Hauran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauran) plain of Syria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECordesman2008222–223-297]

### Size

The plateau's north–south length is approximately 65 km (40 mi) and its east–west width varies from 12 to 25 km (7.5 to 15.5 mi).[^cite_note-ESM-298][^cite_note-299]

Israel has captured, according to its own data, 1,150 km<sup>2</sup> (440 mi<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-300] According to Syria, the Golan Heights measures 1,860 km<sup>2</sup> (718 mi<sup>2</sup>), of which 1,500 km<sup>2</sup> (580 mi<sup>2</sup>) are occupied by Israel.[^cite_note-301] According to the CIA, Israel holds 1,300 km<sup>2</sup> (500 mi<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-Fact-302]

### Topography



Banyas waterfall at the foot of Mount Hermon

The area is hilly and elevated, overlooking the [Jordan Rift Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Rift_Valley) which contains the [Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee) and the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River), and is itself dominated by the 2,814 m (9,232 ft) tall [Mount Hermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon).[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-Fact-302] The Sea of Galilee at the southwest corner of the plateau[^cite_note-ESM-298] and the [Yarmouk River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarmouk_River) to the south are at elevations well below sea level[^cite_note-Fact-302] (the sea of Galilee at about 200 m (660 ft)).[^cite_note-ESM-298]

[Topographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography), the Golan Heights is a plateau with an average altitude of 1,000 metres (3,300 ft),[^cite_note-Fact-302] rising northwards toward Mount Hermon and sloping down to about 400 m (1,300 ft) elevation along the Yarmouk River in the south.[^cite_note-ESM-298] The steeper, more rugged topography is generally limited to the northern half, including the foothills of Mount Hermon; on the south the plateau is more level.[^cite_note-ESM-298]

There are several small peaks on the Golan Heights, most of them volcanic cones, such as Mount Agas (1,350 m, 4,430 ft), [Mount Dov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Dov)/Jebel Rous (1,529 m, 5,016 ft; northern peak 1,524 m, 5,000 ft),[^cite_note-304] Mount Bental (1,171 m, 3,842 ft) and opposite it Mount Avital (1,204 m, 3,950 ft), Mount Ram (1,188 m, 3,898 ft), and [Tal Saki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tal_Saki) (594 metres, 1,949 ft).

#### Subdivisions

The broader Golan plateau exhibits a more subdued topography, generally ranging between 120 and 520 m (390 and 1,710 ft) in elevation. In Israel, the Golan plateau is divided into three regions: northern (between the Sa'ar and Jilabun valleys), central (between the Jilabun and [Daliyot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daliyot_River) valleys), and southern (between the Daliyot and Yarmouk valleys). The Golan Heights is bordered on the west by a rock escarpment that drops 500 m (1,600 ft) to the [Jordan River valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River) and the [Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee). In the south, the incised Yarmouk River valley marks the limits of the plateau and, east of the abandoned railroad bridge upstream of [Hamat Gader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamat_Gader) and [Al Hammah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Hammah), it marks the recognised international border between Syria and Jordan.[^cite_note-305]

### Climate and hydrology

In addition to its strategic military importance, the Golan Heights is an important [water resource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resource), especially at the higher elevations, which are snow-covered in the winter and help sustain [baseflow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baseflow) for rivers and springs during the dry season. The Heights receive significantly more precipitation than the surrounding, lower-elevation areas. The occupied sector of the Golan Heights provides or controls a substantial portion of the water in the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River) [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin), which in turn provides a portion of Israel's water supply. The Golan Heights supplies 15% of Israel's water.[^cite_note-306]



Panorama looking west from the former Syrian post of [Tel Faher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Faher)

A field with a large hill in the background

Panoramic view of the Golan Heights, with the Hermon mountains on the left side, taken from Snir



Panorama showing the upper Golan Heights and Mount Hermon, with the Hula Valley to the left

## Territorial claims

Main article: [Israeli occupation of the Golan Heights](60364960.md)

Claims on the territory include the Franco-British boundary agreement of 1920, which placed portions of the northwestern Golan region, delineated by a rough triangle formed by the towns of [Banias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banias), [Quneitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra) and the northern tip of the [Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee), within the British Palestine Mandate in which the establishment of a Jewish national home had been promised.[^cite_note-EDM35-307][^cite_note-308] As part of the 1923 [Paulet–Newcombe Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paulet%E2%80%93Newcombe_Agreement), this triangle in northwestern Golan was ceded to the French Mandate of Lebanon and Syria in exchange for land areas that had previously been part of the French Mandate, including the whole of the Sea of Galilee, which previously had its eastern boundary connected to the French Mandate and was placed under British control inside the Palestine Mandate.[^cite_note-Garfinkle1998-309]<sup>: 135–146 </sup>[^cite_note-310]

Syria argues that the region was placed in the [Vilayet of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilayet_of_Damascus) as part of Syria under the Ottoman boundaries and that the 1920 agreement was only temporary. Syria further claims that the final border line drawn in 1923, which excluded the Golan triangle, had superseded the 1920 agreement,[^cite_note-EDM35-307] although Syria has never recognised the 1923 border as legally binding.

Israel considers the Golan Heights vital for its national security, asserting that control over the region is necessary to defend against threats from Syria and [Iranian proxy groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_and_state-sponsored_terrorism).[^cite_note-311] It maintains that it may retain the area, as the text of [Resolution 242](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolution_242) calls for "safe and recognised boundaries free from threats or acts of force".[^cite_note-YZB-312]

### Borders, armistice line and ceasefire line



View of [Mount Hermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon) from the road to Masaade

One of the aspects of the dispute involves the existence prior to 1967 of three different lines separating Syria from the area that before 1948 was referred to as [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine).

The 1923 boundary between British [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) and the [French Mandate of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Mandate_of_Syria) was drawn with an objective of providing the water supply needed for the British Mandate.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-Hof-314] Accordingly, it was demarcated so that all of the [Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee), including a 10-metre (33 ft) wide strip of beach along its northeastern shore, would stay inside [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine). From the Sea of Galilee north to [Lake Hula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Hula) the boundary was drawn between 50 and 400 metres (160 and 1,310 ft) east of the upper [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River), keeping that stream entirely within [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine). The British also received a sliver of land along the [Yarmouk River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarmouk_River), out to the present-day [Hamat Gader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamat_Gader).[^cite_note-Garfinkle1998-309]<sup>: 126–148 </sup>

During the Arab–Israeli War, Syria captured various areas of the formerly British controlled [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), including the 10-metre (33 ft) strip of beach, the east bank of the upper Jordan, as well as areas along the Yarmouk.[^cite_note-315]

While negotiating the [1949 Armistice Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_Agreements), Israel called for the removal of all Syrian forces from the territory of the former Palestine Mandate. Syria refused, insisting on an armistice line based not on the 1923 international border but on the military status quo. The result was a compromise. Under the terms of an armistice signed on 20 July 1949, Syrian forces were to withdraw east of the 1923 Anglo-French Palestine-Syria boundary. Israeli forces were to refrain from entering the evacuated areas, which would become a demilitarized zone, "from which the armed forces of both Parties shall be totally excluded, and in which no activities by military or paramilitary forces shall be permitted."[^cite_note-316]

Accordingly, major parts of the armistice lines departed from the 1923 boundary. There were three distinct, non-contiguous enclaves—to the west of Banias, on the west bank of the Jordan River near Lake Hula, and the eastern-southeastern shores of the Sea of Galilee extending out to Hamat Gader, consisting of 66.5 km<sup>2</sup> (25.7 mi<sup>2</sup>) of land lying between the 1949 armistice line and the 1923 boundary, forming the demilitarized zone.[^cite_note-Hof-314]

Following the armistice, both Israel and Syria sought to take advantage of the territorial ambiguities left in place by the 1949 agreement. This resulted in an evolving tactical situation, one "snapshot" of which was the disposition of forces immediately prior to the [Six-Day War](29328.md), the "line of June 4, 1967".[^cite_note-Hof-314]

### Shebaa Farms

A small portion of territory in the Golan Heights, on the Lebanon–Syria border, has been a particular flashpoint. The territory, known as the [Shebaa Farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shebaa_Farms), measures only 22 km<sup>2</sup> (8.5 sq mi). Since 2000, Lebanon has officially claimed it to be Lebanese territory from which Israel should withdraw, and Syria has concurred.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319]

The approximate boundary between Lebanon and Syria has its origins in an 1862 French map.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] During the early period of the [French Mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_for_Syria_and_the_Lebanon), both French and British maps were inconsistent regarding the boundary in the western Golan region, with some showing the Shebaa Farms in Lebanon and others, the majority, showing them in Syria.[^cite_note-322] However, by 1936 the disagreement was eliminated by high quality maps showing the Shebaa Farms in Syria, and these formed the basis of later official maps.[^cite_note-Kaufman36-323] According to Kaufman, the choice between the two options was due to a preference for drawing boundaries along [watersheds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_divide) rather than along valleys.[^cite_note-Kaufman36-323] However, no detailed delineation or demarcation was performed throughout the mandate period.

Meanwhile, problems were reported with the location of the boundary.[^cite_note-Kaufman37-324] Several official documents from the 1930s state that the boundary lies along the Wadi al-'Asal (to the south of the Shebaa Farms).[^cite_note-Kaufman37-324] Local officials of the French administration reported that the de facto boundary did not correspond to the boundary shown on maps.[^cite_note-Kaufman37-324] The [High Commissioner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner) requested a Syrian–Lebanese negotiation but apparently nothing happened.[^cite_note-Kaufman37-324]

From the founding of the Syrian Republic in 1946 until the Israeli occupation in 1967, the Shebaa Farms were controlled by Syria and Lebanon did not make any known official complaint.[^cite_note-Kaufman1-325][^cite_note-LGL-326] The Israel occupation cut off the access of many Lebanese residents from the farms they had worked.[^cite_note-LGL-326] In the context of renewal of the UNIFIL mandate, the Lebanese government implicitly endorsed United Nations maps of the region in 1978 and many times later, even though the maps showed the Shebaa Farms in Syria.[^cite_note-Kaufman1-325]

Lebanese newspapers, residents and politicians lobbied the Lebanese government in the early 1980s to take up the issue, but it was apparently not raised in the failed negotiations for an Israeli withdrawal after the [1982 Israeli invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Lebanon_War).[^cite_note-Kaufman171-327] A series of publications appeared, partly assisted by [Hezbollah](13919.md) and [Amal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amal_Movement), and a committee which formed in the Lebanese town of [Shebaa](42907053.md) wrote to the UN in 1986 protesting Israeli occupation of their lands.[^cite_note-Kaufman171-327] However, it was Hezbollah in 2000 which first adopted the Shebaa Farms as the basis for a public territorial claim against Israel.[^cite_note-328]

On 7 June 2000, the United Nations published the [*Blue Line*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Line_\(Lebanon\)) as the line to which Israel should withdraw from Lebanon in accordance with [Security Council Resolution 425](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_425). The UN chose to follow the maps at its disposal and did not accept the Lebanese complaint from several weeks earlier that the Shebaa Farms were in Lebanon.[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330] After the Israeli withdrawal, the United Nations affirmed on 18 June 2000 that Israel had withdrawn its forces from Lebanon.[^cite_note-SC2000press-331] However, the press release noted that both Lebanon and Syria disagreed, considering the Shebaa Farms area to be Lebanese.[^cite_note-SC2000press-331] In deference to the Lebanese position, the Blue Line is not marked on the ground in this location.[^cite_note-UNIFILkit-332]

The attitude of the UN shifted during the following years. In 2006, the Lebanese government presented the UN with a seven-point plan that included a proposal to place the Shebaa Farms under UN administration until boundary demarcation and sovereignty were settled.[^cite_note-Kaufman212-333] In August of that year, the Security Council passed [Resolution 1701](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1701) which "took due note" of the Lebanese plan and called for "delineation of the international borders of Lebanon, especially in those areas where the border is disputed or uncertain, including by dealing with the Shebaa farms area".[^cite_note-Kaufman212-333][^cite_note-334]

In 2007, a UN cartographer delineated the boundaries of the region: "starting from the turning point of the 1920 French line located just south of the village of El Majidiye; from there continuing south-east along the 1946 Moughr Shab'a-Shab'a boundary until reaching the [thalweg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalweg) of the Wadi al-Aasal; thence following the thalweg of the wadi north-east until reaching the crest of the mountain north of the former hamlet Mazraat Barakhta and reconnecting with the 1920 line."[^cite_note-335] As of 2023, neither Syria nor Israel have responded to the delineation, nor have Lebanon and Syria made progress towards border demarcation.[^cite_note-S-2023-879-336]

The position of Israel, which occupied the Golan Heights in 1967 and annexed them in 1981, is that the Shebaa Farms belonged to Syria and there is no case for Lebanese sovereignty.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-UNIFILkit-332]

### Ghajar

The [Alawite](436616.md) village of [Ghajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghajar) is located west of [Shebaa farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shebaa_farms). Before the [1967 war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six-day_war), the village was located at the junction of Syria, Lebanon and Israel. During the war, Israel did not initially capture the village, since they considered it Lebanese, but Lebanon told a delegation from Ghajar that they would shoot or jail Ghajar residents who crossed the [Hasbani River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasbani_River). Ghajar residents then requested to become part of Israel and accepted Israeli citizenship after the area was annexed in 1981.[^cite_note-Time2015-338] Residents of Ghajar have been integrated in Israeli society and speak fluent Hebrew.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-Ghj-340] The village had been divided by an [international boundary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Line_\(Lebanon\)), though there was no physical border running through it. Instead, the village is surrounded by a fence. The northern part of the village had been on the Lebanese side after the Israeli withdrawal in 2000 following the end of the [South Lebanon conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lebanon_conflict_\(1985%E2%80%932000\)).[^cite_note-Time2015-338] Since July 2006, both halves of the village of 2,450 have been under Israeli control, with a majority of residents opposing a return to Lebanese control.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] There is an [Israeli army](39237.md) checkpoint at the entrance to the village from the rest of the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-LGL-326] Most residents hold dual Israeli citizenship and [Syrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_nationality_law) citizenship,[^cite_note-343] and in the northern part many hold a Lebanese passport.[^cite_note-344]

### International views

The international community largely considers the Golan to be Syrian territory held under Israeli occupation.[^cite_note-ILO-1][^cite_note-InternationalCommunityOccupiedTerritory-345]



The entrance to the Israeli settlement of [Trump Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Heights), founded in 2019, and named after Israeli settlers in tribute to US President Donald Trump

On 25 March 2019, then-President of the United States [Donald Trump](4848272.md) proclaimed [U.S. recognition of the Golan Heights as part of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_recognition_of_the_Golan_Heights_as_part_of_Israel), making it the first country to do so.[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347] Israeli officials lobbied the United States into recognizing "Israeli sovereignty" over the territory.[^cite_note-p589-348] The 28 member states of the [European Union](9317.md) declared in turn that they do not recognize Israeli sovereignty, and several experts on international law reiterated that the principle remains that land gained by either defensive or offensive wars cannot be legally annexed under international law.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] The European members of the UN Security Council issued a joint statement condemning the U.S. announcement and the UN Secretary-General issued a statement saying that the status of the Golan had not changed.[^cite_note-352]

Under the subsequent [administration of President Joe Biden](65732174.md), the [U.S. State Department's](31975.md) annual report on human rights violations around the world again referred to the [West Bank](33209.md), [Gaza Strip](12047.md), [East Jerusalem](343508.md) and the Golan Heights as being territories occupied by Israel.[^cite_note-353] However, in June 2021, the Biden administration affirmed that it would maintain the previous administration's policy of recognizing Israeli sovereignty over the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-354]

On 10 August 2026, Colombia became the second country to recognize Israeli sovereignty over the Golan Heights, after a meeting between the Israeli foreign minister and Colombia's new government.[^cite_note-d869-355][^cite_note-colombia-20] The Israeli foreign minister also stated that he will continue to work for other countries to recognize "Israeli sovereignty" over the territory.[^cite_note-d869-355] Colombia's recognition was condemned by Syria,[^cite_note-b555-356] Saudi Arabia,[^cite_note-k247-357] Egypt, Jordan, Qatar,[^cite_note-l016-358] Kuwait and Iraq.[^cite_note-s674-359] The Secretary-General of the Gulf Cooperation Council issued a statement condemning Colombia's recognition and stated that it violates international law.[^cite_note-m647-360] The United Nations Deputy Special Envoy for Syria Claudio Cordone stated that: "As most recently reaffirmed by Secretary-General António Guterres in Damascus, the Golan Heights are occupied Syrian territory. Colombia's acceptance of Israel's annexation of this territory directly contravenes international law."[^cite_note-w752-361]

## UNDOF supervision



Golan ceasefire line crossing, 2012



A UN [Toyota Land Cruiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota_Land_Cruiser) parked near [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams) displaying UNDOF plates and a UN flag, January 2012

The [United Nations Disengagement Observer Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Disengagement_Observer_Force) (UNDOF) was established in 1974 to supervise the implementation of the [Agreement on Disengagement](40148472.md) and maintain the ceasefire with an area of separation known as the [UNDOF Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDOF_Zone).[^cite_note-362] Currently there are more than 1,000 [UN peacekeepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_UN_peacekeeping_missions) there trying to sustain a peace.[^cite_note-363] Syria and Israel still contest the ownership of the Heights but have not used overt military force since 1974.

The great strategic value of the Heights, both militarily and as a source of water, means that a deal is uncertain. Members of the UN Disengagement force are usually the only individuals who cross the Israeli–Syrian de facto border (cease fire ["Alpha Line"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Disengagement_Observer_Force_Zone#UNDOF_Zone)). Since 1967, Druze brides have been allowed to cross into Syria, although they do so in the knowledge that they may not be able to return.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-JP2009-365] Since 1988, Israel has allowed several hundred Druze pilgrims each year to cross into Syria to visit the shrine of [Abel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cain_and_Abel) on [Mount Qasioun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Qasioun) overlooking [Damascus](8914.md), in visits coordinated through the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md).[^cite_note-JP2009-365]

Though the cease fire in the UNDOF zone has been largely uninterrupted since the seventies, in 2012 there were repeated violations from the Syrian side, including tanks[^cite_note-366] and live gunfire,[^cite_note-367] though these incidents are attributed to the ongoing [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md) rather than intentionally directed towards Israel.[^cite_note-368] On 15 October 2018, the [Quneitra border crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra_Crossing) between the Golan Heights and Syria reopened for UNDOF personnel after four years of closure.[^cite_note-369]

## Syrian villages

Main article: [Syrian towns and villages depopulated in the Arab–Israeli conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_towns_and_villages_depopulated_in_the_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_conflict)

See also: [1982 Golan Heights Druze general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Golan_Heights_Druze_general_strike)



[Beer Ajam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_Ajam) ([بئرعجم](https://ar.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D8%A8%D8%A6%D8%B1%20%D8%B9%D8%AC%D9%85)), a [Syrian Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Syria) village in the province of [Quneitra](7445647.md), founded in 1872



Destroyed buildings in Quneitra

The population of the Golan Heights prior to the 1967 Six-Day War has been estimated between 130,000 and 145,000, including 17,000 Palestinian refugees registered with UNRWA.[^cite_note-HaaretzFogelman-370] Between 80,000[^cite_note-MORRIS-217] and 130,000[^cite_note-almarsad-218] Syrians fled or were driven from the Heights during the Six-Day War and around 7,000 remained in the Israeli-held territory in six villages: [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams), [Mas'ade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mas'ade), [Buq'ata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buq'ata), [Ein Qiniyye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_Qiniyye), [Ghajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghajar) and [Shayta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shayta).[^cite_note-almarsad-218]



Christian church in Ein Qiniyye

Before the [1967 war](29328.md), Christians comprised 12% of the total population of the Golan Heights. The vast majority of [Christians migrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_emigration) with the rest of the population after Israel's occupation of the Golan, leaving only a few small Christian families in Majdal Shams and Ein Qiniyye.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]

Israel forcibly expelled Syrians from the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] There were also instances of Israeli soldiers killing Syrian residents including blowing up their home with people inside.[^cite_note-375]

Israel demolished over one hundred Syrian villages and farms in the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-shai-377] After the demolitions, the lands were given to Israeli settlers.[^cite_note-378]

[Quneitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra) was the largest town in the Golan Heights until 1967, with a population of 27,000. It was occupied by Israel on the last day of the Six-Day War and handed back to Syrian civil control per the 1974 Disengagement Agreement. But the Israelis had destroyed Quneitra with dynamite and bulldozers before they withdrew from the city.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

East of the 1973 ceasefire line, in the Syrian controlled part of the Golan Heights, an area of 600 km<sup>2</sup> (232 sq mi), are more than 40 Syrian towns and villages, including [Quneitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quneitra), [Khan Arnabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_Arnabah), al-Hamidiyah, [al-Rafid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Rafid,_Syria), al-Samdaniyah, al-Mudariyah, [Beer Ajam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_Ajam), [Bariqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bariqa), Ghadir al-Bustan, [Hader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hader,_Quneitra_Governorate), Juba, Kodana, Ufaniyah, Ruwayhinah, Nabe' al-Sakhar, Trinjah, Umm al-A'zam, and Umm Batna. The population of the [Quneitra Governorate](7445647.md) numbers 79,000.[^cite_note-SanaQuneitraGovPop-230]

Once annexing the Golan Heights in 1981, the Israeli government offered all non-Israelis living in the Golan citizenship, but until the early 21st century fewer than 10% of the Druze were Israeli citizens; the remainder held Syrian citizenship.[^cite_note-381] The Golan [Alawites](436616.md) in the village of [Ghajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghajar) accepted Israeli citizenship in 1981.[^cite_note-Ghj-340] In 1982, the Druze community of the Golan Heights held a [five-month general strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Golan_Heights_Druze_general_strike) protesting the Israeli annexation.



The [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) town of [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams)

The Druze living in the Golan Heights are permanent residents of Israel. They hold [laissez-passer documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_document) issued by the Israeli government, and enjoy the country's social-welfare benefits.[^cite_note-382] The pro-Israeli Druze were historically ostracized by the pro-Syrian Druze.[^cite_note-YNet-383] Reluctance to accept citizenship also reflects fear of ill treatment or displacement by Syrian authorities should the Golan Heights be returned to Syria.[^cite_note-384] In the 21st century, the number of Druze accepting Israeli citizenship steadily increased; as of 8 March 2026, 10,323 (39.6%) of the 26,066 residents of the four Druze villages of the Israeli-administered Golan Heights were Israeli citizens.[^cite_note-TOI150326-16]

According to *[The Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Independent)*, most Druze in the Golan Heights live relatively comfortable lives in a freer society under Israeli rule than they would have in Syria under [Assad's government](57134800.md).[^cite_note-385] According to Egypt's *[Daily Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Star_\(Egypt\))*, their standard of living vastly surpasses that of their counterparts on the Syrian side of the border. Most of them identify themselves as Syrian,[^cite_note-386] but feel alienated from the "[autocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy)" government in Damascus and therefore fear of a return to Syrian rule. According to the [Associated Press](18935732.md), "many young Druse have been quietly relieved at the failure of previous Syrian–Israeli peace talks to go forward."[^cite_note-WPost-387]

On the other hand, expressing pro-Syrian viewpoint, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* represents the Golan Druzes' view that by doing so they may be potentially rewarded by Syria, while simultaneously risking nothing in Israel's freewheeling society. *The Economist* likewise reported that "Some optimists see the future Golan as a sort of Hong Kong, continuing to enjoy the perks of Israel's dynamic economy and [open society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_society), while coming back under the sovereignty of a [stricter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), less developed Syria." The Druze are also reportedly well-educated and relatively prosperous, and have made use of Israel's universities.[^cite_note-388]

Since 1988, Druze clerics have been permitted to make annual religious pilgrimages to Syria. Since 2005, Israel has allowed Druze farmers to export some 11,000 tons of apples to the rest of Syria each year, constituting the first commercial relations between Syria and Israel.[^cite_note-WPost-387]

In the first years after the onset of the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md), the number of applications for Israeli citizenship grew, although Syrian loyalty remained strong and those who applied for citizenship were often ostracized by members of the older generation.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] In 2012, there were 20,000 [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) with Syrian citizenship living in the Israeli-occupied portion of the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-391] In recent years, the number of applications for citizenship has increased, 239 in 2021 and 206 in the first half of 2022. A total of 419 citizenship applications were approved in 2022. In 2023, a further 389 citizenship applications were granted, followed by 318 in the first 11 months of 2024. By the end of 2024, official Israeli figures suggest that of approximately 29,000 Druze living in the Golan Heights, about 6,000 (or 20.45 percent) were Israeli citizens.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] In 2025 and early 2026, applications for Israeli citizenship surged sharply among Druze residents of the Golan Heights. In March 2026, *Le Monde* reported that, according to an anonymous Druze community observer based in Majdal Shams, before 2023 only about one-third of the Golan Druze held Israeli citizenship, but that the share had since risen to more than half.[^cite_note-394] In January 2025, the IDF recorded the first large-scale enlistment of Golan Druze, with about 120 adults joining, marking a notable increase in recruitment.[^cite_note-395]

## Israeli settlements

See also: [List of Israeli settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_settlements) and [Israeli occupation of the Golan Heights](60364960.md)



Map of the [Israeli settlements](15123.md) in the Golan Heights, 1992

[Israeli settlement](15123.md) activity began in the 1970s. The area was governed by military administration until 1981, when Israel passed the [Golan Heights Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Heights_Law), extending [Israeli law](8932627.md) and administration throughout the territory.[^cite_note-MFALaw-35] This move was condemned by the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) in [UN Resolution 497](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council_Resolution_497),[^cite_note-condemn-2] although Israel states it has a right to retain the area, citing the text of [UN Resolution 242](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Council_Resolution_242), adopted after the Six-Day War, which calls for "safe and recognised boundaries free from threats or acts of force".[^cite_note-YZB-312] The continued Israeli control of the Golan Heights remains controversial and is still regarded as an occupation by most countries other than [Israel](9282173.md) and the [United States](3434750.md). Israeli settlements and human rights policy in the occupied territory have drawn criticism from the UN.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]



The Israeli settlement of [Ma'ale Gamla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ale_Gamla)



Israeli farms in the Golan Heights

The territory is administered by the [Golan Regional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Regional_Council), based in [Katzrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katzrin), which has a population of 7,600. There are another 19 [moshavim](1597952.md) and 10 [kibbutzim](106200.md). In 1989, the Israeli settler population was 10,000,[^cite_note-398] doubling itself by 2010[^cite_note-BBCGH-399] in a total of 32 settlements.[^cite_note-OUDAT-400][^cite_note-401] By 2019, the population reached 22,000,[^cite_note-402] and was estimated to be 25,000 in 2021, with plans by the Government of Prime Minister [Naftali Bennett](39754840.md) to double that population over a five-year period.[^cite_note-b197-12]

On 23 April 2019, Israel Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) announced that he would bring a resolution for government approval to name a new community in the Golan Heights after U.S. President Donald Trump.[^cite_note-403] The planned settlement was unveiled as [Trump Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Heights) on 16 June 2019.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] Further plans for settlement expansion on the Golan were part of the agenda of Benjamin Netanyahu's incoming coalition in 2023.[^cite_note-406]

In December 2024, Prime Minister Netanyahu announced an updated plan to further expand settlements on the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-407] As of the end of 2024, the Israeli settler population was estimated to be about 31,000 people.[^cite_note-Reuters101224-15]

In August 2026, the Dutch government banned products from Israeli settlements in the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-t912-408]

## Landmarks

The Golan Heights features numerous archeological sites, mountains, streams and waterfalls. Throughout the region, 25 ancient synagogues have been found, dating back to the Roman and Byzantine periods.[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410]

- Shrine of [Abu Dharr al-Ghifari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dharr_al-Ghifari), early Islamic era, located in the eastern Golan.

  [^cite_note-l370-411]

  [^cite_note-i641-412]

- [Banias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banias) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): بانياس الحولة; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): בניאס) is an ancient site that developed around a spring once associated with the Greek god [Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_\(god\)). Near the archaeological site is the Banias Waterfall, one of the most powerful waterfalls in the region, plunging about 10 meters into a pool surrounded by lush vegetation. Part of the stream is accessible via a 100-meter-long suspended walkway.

  [^cite_note-413]

- [Deir Qeruh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_Qeruh) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): دير قروح; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): דיר קרוח) is a ruined [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine)-period and Syrian village. Founded in the 4th century CE, it has a monastery and church of [St George](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_George) from the 6th century. The church has a square apse – a feature known from ancient Syria and Jordan but not present in churches west of the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River).

  [^cite_note-JMOC-146]

- [Kursi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kursi,_Sea_of_Galilee) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): الكرسي; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): כורסי) is an archaeological site and national park on the shore of the Sea of Galilee at the foothills of the Golan, containing the ruins of a Byzantine Christian monastery and is traditionally identified as the location of [Jesus](1095706.md)' [Miracle of the Swine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracle_of_the_Swine) ([Gergesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gergesa)).

  [^cite_note-414]

- [Katzrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katzrin) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): قصرين; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): קצרין) is the administrative and commercial center of the Israeli-occupied Golan Heights. [Katzrin Ancient Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katzrin_Ancient_Village) is an archaeological site on the outskirts of Katzrin where the remains of a [Talmud-era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbinic_period) village and synagogue have been reconstructed.

  [^cite_note-415]

  &#x20;The site has been described by an archeologist as being developed: "with a clear agenda and nationalistic narrative."

  [^cite_note-416]

  &#x20;It has also been criticized for distorting historical items and showing a selective part of history, focusing on the Jewish period leaving out the Mamluk and Syrian periods.

  [^cite_note-417]

  [^cite_note-418]

  &#x20;[Golan Archaeological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Archaeological_Museum) hosts archaeological finds uncovered in the Golan Heights from prehistoric times. A special focus concerns Gamla and excavations of synagogues and Byzantine churches.

  [^cite_note-419]



The Sea of Galilee as seen from the Golan

- [Gamla nature reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamla_nature_reserve) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): שמורת טבע גמלא) is an open park with the archaeological remains of the ancient Jewish city of [Gamla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamla) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): גמלא, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): جمالا) — including a tower, wall and synagogue. It is also the site of a large waterfall, an ancient Byzantine church, and a panoramic spot to observe the nearly 100 vultures that dwell in the cliffs. Israeli scientists study the vultures and tourists can watch them fly and nest.

  [^cite_note-420]

- A [ski resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon_ski_resort) on the slopes of [Mount Hermon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Hermon) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): جبل الشيخ; הר חרמון) features a wide range of ski trails and activities. The first lift was installed in 1971 and the resort first opened to skiers in December 1971.

  [^cite_note-421]

  &#x20;Since 1972, the resort has been operated and held by the residents of the nearby [Moshav](1597952.md) of [Neve Ativ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neve_Ativ).

  [^cite_note-422]

  &#x20;The [Lake Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Ram) [crater lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_crater_lake) is nearby.



Hippos [odeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odeon_\(building\))

- [Hippos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippos_\(Golan_Heights\)) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): قلعة الحصن; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): סוסיתא) is an ancient Greco-Roman city, known in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) as *Qal'at al-Hisn* and in [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic) as *Susita*. The archaeological site includes excavations of the city's forum, the small imperial cult temple, a large Hellenistic temple compound, the Roman city gates, and two Byzantine churches.

- The [Nimrod Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimrod_Castle) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): لعة الصبيبة; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): מבצר נמרוד) was built in the 13th century to defend against the [Crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades), served the [Ayyubids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubids) and [Mamluks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluks), and was captured only once, in 1260, by the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongols). It is now located inside a nature reserve.

  [^cite_note-423]



Rujm el-Hiri

- [Rujm el-Hiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rujm_el-Hiri) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): رجم الهري; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): גלגל רפאים) is a large circular stone monument. Excavations since 1968 have not uncovered material remains common to archaeological sites in the region. Archaeologists believe the site may have been a ritual center linked to a cult of the dead.

  [^cite_note-424]

  &#x20;A 3D model of the site exists in the Museum of Golan Antiquities in Katzrin.

- [Senaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Har_Senaim) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): جبل الحلاوة; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): הר סנאים) is an [archaeological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological) site in northern Golan Heights that includes both [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_temple) and [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) temples. [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) and [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk) coins have also been found at this site.

- [Tell Hadar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_Hadar) ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): תל הדר) is an [Aramean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramean) [archaeological site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_site).

- [Umm el-Qanatir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_el-Qanatir) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): ام القناطر; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): עין קשתות, *Ein Keshatot*) is another impressive set of standing ruins of a village of the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) era. The site includes a very large synagogue and two arches next to a natural spring.

  [^cite_note-425]

## Economy

### Viticulture



The vineyard behind the Israeli settlement of [Ma'ale Gamla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ale_Gamla).

On a visit to Israel and the Golan Heights in 1972, Cornelius Ough, a professor of [viticulture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viticulture) and [oenology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oenology) at the [University of California, Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Davis), pronounced conditions in the Golan very suitable for the cultivation of wine grapes.[^cite_note-upstart-426] A consortium of four kibbutzim and four moshavim took up the challenge, clearing 250 burnt-out tanks in the Golan's [Valley of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_of_Tears) to plant vineyards for what would eventually become the [Golan Heights Winery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Heights_Winery).[^cite_note-427] The first vines were planted in 1976, and the first wine was released by the winery in 1983.[^cite_note-upstart-426] As of 2012, The Heights are home to about a dozen wineries.[^cite_note-428]

### Oil and gas exploration

In the early 1990s, the Israel National Oil Company (INOC) was granted [shaft-sinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaft-sinking) permits in the Golan Heights. It estimated a recovery potential of two million barrels of oil, equivalent at the time to \$24 million. During the [Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin) administration (1992–1995), the permits were suspended as efforts were undertaken to restart peace negotiations between Israel and Syria. In 1996, Benjamin Netanyahu granted preliminary approval to INOC to proceed with oil exploration drilling in the Golan.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431]

INOC began undergoing a process of privatization in 1997, overseen by then-Director of the Government Companies Authority (GCA), [Tzipi Livni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tzipi_Livni). During that time, it was decided that INOC's drilling permits would be returned to the state.[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433] In 2012, National Infrastructure Minister [Uzi Landau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzi_Landau) approved exploratory drilling for oil and natural gas in the Golan.[^cite_note-434] The following year, the Petroleum Council of Israel's [Ministry of Energy and Water Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_and_Water_Resources_Ministry) secretly awarded a drilling license covering half the area of the Golan Heights to a local subsidiary of [New Jersey](21648.md)–based [Genie Energy Ltd.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genie_Energy_Ltd.) headed by [Effi Eitam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effi_Eitam).[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436]

Human rights groups have said that the drilling violates international law, as the Golan Heights are an occupied territory.[^cite_note-437]

On 18 November 2021, the United Nations Second Committee approved a draft resolution that demanded that: "Israel, the occupying Power, cease the exploitation, damage, cause of loss or depletion and endangerment of the natural resources in the Occupied Palestinian Territory, including East Jerusalem, and in the occupied Syrian Golan".[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

[^cite_note-AB-116]: &#x20;Berlin, Andrea (2006). *Gamla I: The Pottery of the Second Temple Period*. Israel Antiquities Authority. pp. 153–154. During the first century BCE, under Hasmonean encouragement, Judean Jews had moved north to Galilee and Gaulanitis. ... Meanwhile, between their protected spur in central Gaulanitis, the Hasmonean kingdom imploded and Herod rose to power. At his death in 4 BCE, two of his sons, Herod Philip and Herod Antipas, took over Gaulanitis and Galilee respectively.

[^cite_note-MAC-117]: &#x20;Chancey, Mark Alan; Porter, Adam Lowry (2001). ["The Archaeology of Roman Palestine"](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.2307/3210829). *Near Eastern Archaeology*. **64** (4): 164–203. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.2307/3210829](https://doi.org/10.2307%2F3210829). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [1094-2076](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/1094-2076). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [3210829](https://www.jstor.org/stable/3210829). The Jewish presence probably dated back to the mid second century BCE, and most likely increased after the Hasmonean conquest of the region during the reign of Alexander Jannaeus. Itureans were among the earliest settlers in this period, having also arrived in the mid second century BCE, and ceramic evidence at numerous sites demonstrates that they continued to live there in the Roman Period. ... The initial Roman campaigns occurred in Galilee and adjacent parts of the Golan, and military activities are visible in the archaeological records of several sites. Though Gamala initially remained loyal to Agrippa II in the Jewish Revolt, it ultimately chose to rebel. Wartime coins unique to the city bear the Hebrew inscription "for the redemption of H\[oly] Jerusalem" (Syon 1992/93). The town, surrounded by steep ravines, repelled assaults by the loyalist forces of Agrippa but could not withstand the protracted siege by Roman troops that followed. Josephus described the battle in epic terms: to escape capture, "multitudes plunged headlong with their wives and children into the ravine which had been excavated to a vast depth beneath the citadel" (War 4.80). The Roman breach of the wall by the synagogue is still visible today, and fortress walls, remains of towers, pieces of armor, arrowheads, sling stones, ballista stones, and traces of fire attest to the ferocity of the siege.

Accordingly, the Arab states have to prepare the plans necessary for dealing with the political, economic and social aspects, so that if necessary results are not achieved, collective Arab military preparations, when they are not completed, will constitute the ultimate practical means for the final liquidation of Israel

Accessed April 10, 2026. "The territorial aspects of the Syrian-Israeli dispute date to 1920–23, when Great Britain and France devised a boundary between Syria (then including “Greater Lebanon”) and Palestine, two entities that would fall under League of Nations mandates. Often referred to as the '1923 international boundary,' the line was drawn to keep the upper course of the Jordan River (between Lake Hula and the Sea of Galilee) and the Sea of Galilee itself entirely within Palestine and to give Palestine a few kilometers of frontage on the Yarmouk River (see map, page 5). Between Lake Hula and the Sea of Galilee, the boundary ran between fifty and four-hundred meters east of the Jordan River, just below the Golan Heights."

["Inside the Village Caught in the Crossfire Between Israel, Syria and Lebanon"](https://time.com/3815086/ghajar-golan-crossfire/), *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*, April 10, 2015. Accessed April 4, 2026. "In 1967, Israel conquered the Golan Heights from Syria and those Ghajar residents who remained later chose to accept Israeli citizenship when the territory was annexed in 1981. In 1982, when Israel occupied southern Lebanon, the village expanded into Lebanese territory. When Israel pulled out of southern Lebanon in 2000, the residents in northern Ghajar suddenly found themselves living in Lebanon, whilst their relatives and neighbours who previously lived just across the street now lived in a separate country — and an enemy state.... The division of the village — one half in Israel, the other in Lebanon — lasted until 2006 when Israel invaded north Ghajar during the Second Lebanon War."

[^cite_note-339]: &#x20;["June 12, 2000"](https://www.proquest.com/docview/2233377222). *The New York Times*. [ProQuest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ProQuest) [2233377222](https://www.proquest.com/docview/2233377222). Retrieved 3 April 2026.

Accessed 17 March 2026. "Until 1967, Ghajar was a Syrian village on the Syrian-Lebanese border. In 1967, it was captured by Israel when the IDF took the Golan Heights. Apparently the northern part of the village was then already inside Lebanese territory, but it was only when Israel withdrew from Lebanon in May 2000, that the UN drew the international border between Israel and Lebanon.... But unlike the Druze from the four villages on the Golan Heights that came under Israeli rule in 1967, the identification with Syria in Ghajar is not absolute. The residents agreed to accept Israeli citizenship in 1981.

Some 2,000 people live in Ghajar. Most of the villagers still consider themselves Syrian Alawites, the minority sect which Syrian President Bashar al-Assad is part of. Many have taken Israeli citizenship during the long years of occupation and most residents refuse to be part of Lebanon."

Accessed March 31, 2026. "In an emotional operation with delicate diplomatic underpinnings, over 550 Druse residents of the Golan Heights crossed the border into Syria on Thursday morning for a five-day trip that will include a visit to the tomb of the Prophet Habil, the Biblical Abel, southwest of Damascus. The crossing, which was coordinated among the IDF, the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC), and the Israeli and Syrian interior ministries, was the 14th crossing of its kind in as many years, and included a higher number of pilgrims than had taken part in previous visits.... In 2008 the ICRC facilitated the crossing of 552 students in nine separate operations throughout the year. For 21 years, the ICRC has also assisted with practical arrangements for weddings - including security clearances for wedding guests - between Druse residents of the Golan and their prospective partners from Syria."

[^cite_note-417]: &#x20;[Sulimani & Kletter 2022](#CITEREFSulimaniKletter2022), pp. 63–64: "Readers will not learn that there was also a Mamluk village and a mosque, and will not be able to see their remains. 'Traditional' items taken from the deserted villages (plough yoke, winnowing fork, etc.) seem to demonstrate the ancient Jewish life (Killebrew and Fine 1991: 53); but the visitors are not told about their origins. Years later, Killebrew criticised the politics that shaped the exhibition of Jewish Qatzrin, while erasing Mamluk Kasrein (Killebrew 2010: 130–131; 2019). Establishing museums is a common colonial practice for expropriating the past. The past is researched, published and exhibited, but in selective ways that erase the cultures of the 'natives' (Dietler 2010\:41; Kosasa 2011; Perugini 2017)."

[^cite_note-418]: &#x20;[Boytner, Dodd & Parker 2010](#CITEREFBoytnerDoddParker2010), p. 131: "In retrospect, I have mixed feelings regarding my role in the Qasrin project. My most serious misgiving is that later Islamic periods-the Mamluk and modern Syrian periods-are not presented to the public. For all intents and purposes these periods have been erased from the contemporary landscape. Although the Jewish heritage of Qasrin is certainly one of many legitimate narratives of the past, public presentation of the site intentionally disregards these two other but no less important periods of occupation."
